Recognition of the Inland Lakes’ 1904 priority may reduce the project’s effect if Deer Creek operates junior to it. If Wyoming instead claims authority to operate out of priority, the submitted hydrologic affidavits present a genuine dispute. They defeat summary judgment; they do not establish the demanding injury element at trial. No new injunction issues, and the preexisting decree remains operative while the remaining questions proceed before the Master.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589 and 665 — applied: Paragraphs X and XIII retain distinct functions; ultimate interpretation reserved.
- Idaho ex rel. Evans, 462 U.S. 1017 — applied: new injunctive allocation still needs the demanding injury showing.
- Anderson, 477 U.S. 242 — applied as a guide to the materially disputed project and injury evidence.

#### Prior requirements calculations are not absolute canal caps

**Holding and operative rule:** The 1945 requirements calculations do not impose absolute ceilings on individual or aggregate canal diversions from the pivotal reach; Paragraph V permits Nebraska to allocate its apportioned share among its canals. Paragraph IV’s limits on canal priority against federal reservoir storage remain, and the legal consequence of allegedly excessive calls is reserved pending an adequate factual record.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court, V; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a number used to calculate an interstate allocation is not automatically an operating cap on every canal. The original requirements figures helped determine the appropriate division of natural flows. Paragraph V does not convert them into diversion ceilings and expressly permits Nebraska to allocate its share among canals. Paragraph IV performs a different task: it establishes canal priority relative to federal reservoirs and limits the circumstances in which canals may stop reservoir storage. Preserving that limitation does not require inventing an absolute quantity restriction absent from the decree. Wyoming’s broader allegations about downstream uses and excessive calls may present additional questions, but the present motions lack the developed facts needed to resolve them. Partial summary judgment therefore establishes only the absence of the proposed categorical caps. It supplies no permission for abusive calls, revises no percentage allocation and determines no unsubmitted demand for bypass of natural flow.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589 and 665 — construed: allocation calculations, Paragraph IV priority and Paragraph V canal allocation remain distinct.

Existing Inland Lakes and canal rights clarified April 20; independently sufficient acquiescence holding recorded; enforcement/modification burdens preserved with precise application. Remaining factual and Paragraph X questions stay open.

#### Brecht v. Abrahamson, 1993-04-21

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/brecht_merits_1993-04-21.md).

#### The protected-silence error remains constitutional trial error

**Holding and operative rule:** Using Brecht’s silence after Miranda warnings to impeach his trial explanation violates Doyle’s due-process protection; permitted impeachment with prewarning silence is analytically separate. This violation is trial error subject to harmless-error review, not a merely nonconstitutional rule or a structural defect requiring automatic reversal.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, I; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the warning’s assurance matters. Doyle protects a defendant from being told that he may remain silent and then having that protected silence used to discredit a later explanation. That is a due-process rule of fundamental fairness, not merely another layer of prophylaxis that can be treated as nonconstitutional error. Jenkins and Fletcher permit the impeachment based on silence before the warning that occurred here; the prosecution’s broader references crossed a different line when they reached the protected period. The distinction remains material even though the prosecution also used lawful impeachment. Under Fulminante, an error in presenting the case to the factfinder can be assessed in the context of the rest of the record. This conceded Doyle violation has that character. Recognizing it therefore establishes neither structural error nor an automatic right to a new trial. The remaining inquiry concerns the effect of the constitutional error under the proper allocation of uncertainty.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Doyle, 426 U.S. 610 — applied: warning-induced silence receives due-process protection.
- Jenkins, 447 U.S. 231, and Fletcher, 455 U.S. 603 — distinguished: prewarning impeachment remains permissible in the circumstances presented.
- Fulminante, 499 U.S. 279 — applied: this evidentiary trial error permits harmlessness analysis; structural defects remain distinct.

#### Chapman governs this preserved Doyle claim on habeas

**Holding and operative rule:** For this preserved Doyle trial error properly before the federal habeas court, the State must establish beyond a reasonable doubt that the constitutional error did not contribute to the verdict. Collateral posture alone does not replace that burden with Kotteakos; the reviewing court must apply Chapman to the whole record, with relief conditional on failure of the State’s showing rather than automatic from the violation.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, II–III; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court retains Chapman’s allocation for this constitutional violation. Section 2254 does not expressly choose between the competing harmless-error standards, and prior collateral applications in Rose and Yates do not conclusively settle that precise choice. They nevertheless support continuity with the familiar rule protecting constitutional trial rights. Kotteakos supplies a legitimate alternative model, and finality, federalism and the costs of delayed retrial weigh in its favor. For this properly presented use of protected silence, the Court concludes that uncertainty about contribution to the verdict should remain with the State responsible for the violation. Independent exhaustion, preservation and other habeas requirements continue to protect finality; Chapman itself permits a conviction to stand when harmlessness is proved. The Seventh Circuit must evaluate lawful impeachment and the physical evidence against the credibility defense under that allocation. The Court neither decides harmlessness itself nor reinstates the writ automatically, and it does not prescribe every collateral-review procedure.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Chapman, 386 U.S. 18 — applied on federal habeas to this preserved Doyle claim with its burden unchanged.
- Kotteakos, 328 U.S. 750 — not extended to replace Chapman here; its existing nonconstitutional-error operation remains.
- Rose, 478 U.S. 570, and Yates, 500 U.S. 391 — continuity supported; prior assumed use is not misdescribed as a square holding on this distinction.
- Stringer, Sochor, Espinosa and Richmond — unchanged: actual correction of capital weighing errors remains distinct.
- Sawyer and Herrera — not extended into a universal innocence requirement for review of preserved trial error.

Effective April 21, Chapman expressly governs this preserved Doyle claim on habeas. Kotteakos is not adopted as a general constitutional-habeas standard. Other habeas gates, structural doctrine and capital-weighing correction are unchanged.

#### Withrow v. Williams, 1993-04-21

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/withrow_merits_1993-04-21.md).

#### A fair state opportunity does not itself bar Miranda habeas review

**Holding and operative rule:** Powell’s prudential restriction on Fourth Amendment exclusionary claims does not extend to a state prisoner’s claim that the conviction rests on statements obtained in violation of Miranda. A full and fair state opportunity therefore does not itself foreclose that claim; ordinary habeas requirements, merits analysis and the governing harmless-error inquiry remain necessary.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, I–II; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court preserves the statutory review channel without deciding that every claim succeeds. Powell limited a remedy whose collateral use contributes little additional deterrence to a completed search violation while excluding reliable evidence. Miranda protects the trial privilege against compelled self-incrimination and guards against unreliable statements. Kimmelman confirms that a safeguard tied to a fundamental trial right is not excluded merely because another exclusionary claim would be. The practical case for extending Powell also fails. Prisoners could continue to litigate due-process involuntariness, often requiring a more elaborate inquiry in which warnings remain relevant. Eliminating the structured Miranda inquiry would therefore save little while removing a distinct protection. Calling the safeguard prophylactic does not decide that comparison, and the Court need not resolve every consequence of that label. The claim remains subject to its own merits and remedial requirements. No general reopening of Fourth Amendment claims or exception to the ordinary habeas gates follows.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Powell, 428 U.S. 465 — distinguished and not extended; its actual prudential scope remains.
- Miranda, 384 U.S. 436 — preserved on habeas for a conviction resting on improperly obtained statements.
- Kimmelman, 477 U.S. 365; Jackson, 443 U.S. 307; Rose v. Mitchell, 443 U.S. 545 — applied as limits on extending Powell to distinct trial protections.
- Miller v. Fenton, 474 U.S. 104, and Connelly, 479 U.S. 157 — preserved: independent voluntariness review remains available on its own predicates.

#### An unpleaded, unconsented coercion theory requires fair litigation

**Holding and operative rule:** A habeas court may not grant relief on the independent postwarning involuntariness theory presented here when it was neither pleaded nor tried by express or implied consent and the State lacked an opportunity to present relevant evidence. Litigation of Miranda custody and warning issues does not by itself supply consent; any later presentation remains subject to ordinary pleading, exhaustion, retroactivity and other applicable requirements.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, III; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the two constitutional inquiries cannot be treated as interchangeable pleadings. Whether warnings were required at a particular stage of questioning differs from whether the suspect’s will was overborne under the totality of circumstances. Rule 15(b) allows adjudication of an unpleaded issue actually tried by consent, but the record shows no word or conduct by which the State consented to this independent theory. The absence of a hearing or even argument denied the State a fair chance to offer evidence bearing on coercion and the later statements. Connelly’s police-coercion requirement and the existing totality inquiry cannot be satisfied merely by establishing a warning violation. The resulting voluntariness ruling must therefore be reversed. The Court decides neither that the statements were voluntary nor that Williams can never present the claim. Exhaustion and Teague objections, and the merits of any lawfully presented theory, remain for appropriate proceedings rather than resolution on this deficient process.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Connelly, 479 U.S. 157 — preserved: actual police coercion is necessary to due-process involuntariness; absent warnings alone do not establish it.
- Miller v. Fenton, 474 U.S. 104 — preserved: voluntariness requires its own legal determination on the proper record.
- Teague, 489 U.S. 288 — reserved here; no new exception or merits resolution of the retroactivity objection.

Effective April 21, no Powell bar to the presented Miranda review; distinct notice/consent requirement applied. No automatic writ, coercion finding or universal rule for prophylactic safeguards.

#### CSX Transportation, Inc. v. Easterwood, 1993-04-21

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/easterwood_merits_1993-04-21.md).

#### Coverage requires substantial subsumption of the actual duty

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 434 reaches common-law railroad-safety duties, but preempts them only when operative federal rules substantially subsume the subject of the relevant state requirement, not merely touch it. A covered additional or more stringent requirement survives only when necessary to eliminate or reduce an essentially local safety hazard, not incompatible with federal requirements and not an undue burden on interstate commerce.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, I; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court starts with Congress’s chosen boundary. Common-law duties are state requirements even though a jury rather than a legislature applies them. Cipollone supports that characterization without supplying the scope of this different statute. Section 434 uses covering, a more exacting relationship than mere connection with railroad safety, and surrounds that term with provisions preserving state authority. Rice and Shaw direct attention to text and structure before finding displacement of traditional safety law. The inquiry therefore identifies the actual state duty and asks what the operative federal regulation treats. It is not limited to physical impossibility of simultaneous compliance, and lack of conflict alone cannot save a covered requirement. Congress separately preserved a narrow local-hazard route with three cumulative conditions: necessity for an essentially local hazard, compatibility and no undue commerce burden. Calling ordinary negligence local merely because it concerns one crossing does not satisfy those conditions or erase the coverage inquiry.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Cipollone, June 24, 1992 — applied at its common-law-duty and text-specific scope; its tobacco predicates do not decide FRSA coverage.
- Rice, 331 U.S. 218, and Shaw, 463 U.S. 85 — applied to identify congressional purpose through text and structure.
- Morales, Gade, D.C. Board of Trade and Boston Harbor — distinguished: different statutory language and regulatory settings supply no substitute FRSA test.

#### Warning-device preemption requires the operative project predicates

**Holding and operative rule:** General crossing-safety planning and MUTCD compliance do not displace this warning negligence claim. Sections 646.214(b)(3)–(4) do displace independent device-selection duties when applicable, but planned and abandoned Cook Street gates, redirected funds and circuitry that does not warn motorists do not establish federal participation in installation of warning devices here; the separate subsection (b)(2) highway-project route is not presented.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, II; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a federal program encouraging orderly safety planning does not by itself transfer every railroad duty to the State. Part 924 establishes planning conditions, while the MUTCD describes device standards and shared responsibilities rather than commanding installation at every crossing. The more specific rules in sections 646.214(b)(3) and (4) differ: once their predicates operate, federal requirements or approval replace independent state choices about warning devices. CSX has not established that condition at Cook Street. The planned gate was abandoned and its funds redirected. Motion-detection circuitry installed to serve the West Avenue project was not an active or passive device warning motorists at Cook Street, and work at other crossings did not establish a completed Cook Street installation. The same analysis defeats the asserted implied conflict. The Court leaves negligence and the effect of the city’s decisions for the state-law litigation; it neither orders gates nor finds the railroad liable.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Cipollone, June 24, 1992 — predicate-duty method preserved; this application turns on FRSA and its own regulations.
- Rice, 331 U.S. 218 — applied against inferring displacement from general planning provisions.

#### The ordinary excessive-speed claim is covered

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 213.9’s track-class speed regulation, read in its safety-regulatory setting, covers the pleaded ordinary claim that this train should have traveled more slowly for the time and place despite compliance with the class-four 60-mile-per-hour maximum. That general negligence claim does not qualify merely by its local facts for section 434’s local-hazard saving clause; a duty to slow or stop for a specific individual hazard is not decided.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, III–IV; White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the applicable maximum is part of a federal safety determination, not simply a ceiling leaving ordinary speed limits open to case-by-case substitution. The regulatory system coordinates speed, track conditions and crossing protection, including warnings responsive to train approach and safeguards for higher-speed operations. Section 434 asks whether the rules cover the subject, not whether derailment rather than crossing safety was the Secretary’s principal purpose. Here the allegation is that a train traveling below the class-four maximum was nonetheless too fast for the time and place. That is the ordinary operating-speed duty the regulation covers. The contrary maximum-only reading would permit the same federally treated subject to be redecided through general negligence at each crossing. Particular local facts do not turn that general rule into an essentially local safety-hazard requirement satisfying the statutory conditions. The Court does not decide the separately reserved specific-individual-hazard duty or adjudicate other claims outside the selected review.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Section 213.9 under FRSA — construed to cover ordinary operating-speed negligence in this setting.
- Rice, 331 U.S. 218 — applied through the statutory coverage inquiry; does not defeat demonstrated coverage.
- Cipollone — no extension into a universal preemption rule; actual regulated subject controls.

**Operative regulatory conditions and qualifications:** The device provisions remain distinct. Under §646.214(b)(3)(i), adequate warning devices for a covered project include automatic gates with flashing lights when there are multiple main-line tracks; multiple nearby tracks allowing a train to obscure another; high speed combined with limited sight distance; high speed combined with moderately high highway and rail traffic; high vehicular or train volume, substantial schoolbus or hazardous-material truck traffic, unusually restricted sight distance, continuing accidents, or a combination; or a diagnostic-team recommendation. Under (b)(3)(ii), when a diagnostic team justifies that gates are inappropriate, FHWA may find those requirements inapplicable; that permission is not a command to omit gates. For covered installations to which (b)(3) does not apply, (b)(4) requires FHWA approval of the device choice. These qualifications do not establish that the Cook Street project predicate was met. Section (b)(2)’s separate provision for crossings within or near the terminus of a federal-aid highway construction or improvement project is not adjudicated because CSX did not invoke that predicate here.

Effective April 21, substantial-coverage rule and distinct warning/speed applications established under FRSA. No blanket exemption for crossing negligence, actual-conflict-only test or decision of the reserved individual-hazard issue.

#### United States v. Olano — 1993-04-26

#### Forfeiture, waiver and the limits on plain-error authority

**Holding and operative rule:** Rule 52(b) permits correction of a forfeited legal error only if it is plain and affects substantial rights; valid intentional relinquishment of a known right extinguishes error, while mere failure timely to assert a right does not. Plain means clear or obvious under current law, and the defendant ordinarily must show prejudice affecting the outcome; errors first clarified on appeal, special outcome-independent categories and justified prejudice presumptions are reserved.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that failure to object changes the conditions of appellate relief without making a violated rule disappear. Johnson v. Zerbst supplies the distinction between intentional surrender and omission; whether a right can be waived and what participation or procedures waiver requires depend on the particular right. Young reads Rule 52(b) as a limited power, not two disjunctive routes based on either plainness or substantial rights. Its prejudice inquiry ordinarily examines the record, as harmless-error review does, but places persuasion on the defendant who forfeited the objection. This does not decide that every error demands a specific outcome showing. Fulminante preserves the distinction between trial errors and defects affecting the tribunal’s basic structure. Here the Government’s concessions permit the Court to assume error and plainness without resolving whether Rule 24(c) can be waived. They do not concede prejudice. The preserved constitutional error in Brecht arose under a different review posture and does not supply the burden here.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Johnson v. Zerbst, 304 U.S. 458: applies intentional relinquishment; right-specific waiver requirements remain.
- United States v. Young, 470 U.S. 1: applies limited plain-error authority and ordinary prejudice requirement.
- Arizona v. Fulminante, 499 U.S. 279: preserves structural/trial-error distinction without deciding every Rule 52(b) category.
- Simulated Brecht, April 21, 1993: distinguished; preserved Doyle habeas review does not control forfeited Rule 24(c) error.

#### Correction remains discretionary

**Holding and operative rule:** Even when plain forfeited error affects substantial rights, correction under Rule 52(b) is permitted, not automatic, and should occur only when the error seriously affects the fairness, integrity or public reputation of judicial proceedings. Actual innocence is not a necessary condition, but satisfaction of substantial rights alone does not satisfy this additional inquiry.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the word “may” leaves a separate remedial judgment to the reviewing court. Atkinson supplies its governing measure: the effect on the fairness, integrity or public reputation of judicial proceedings. Young carries that standard into Rule 52(b). Treating every prejudicial plain error as requiring correction would erase the discretion the Rule deliberately retains. The contrary argument that relief must always depend on actual innocence also fails. Innocence can make correction compelling, but serious injury to the administration of justice can exist independently of guilt. The collateral gateway addressed in Sawyer concerns a different procedural obstacle and does not rewrite this appellate rule. These distinctions prevent either an automatic reversal rule or an innocence-only restriction. Because respondents have not satisfied the preceding substantial-rights requirement, the Court does not decide whether this particular intrusion, had it been prejudicial, would warrant the exercise of remedial discretion.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Atkinson, 297 U.S. 157: applies the independent fairness, integrity and public-reputation standard.
- United States v. Young, 470 U.S. 1: applies discretionary, circumscribed correction.
- Simulated Sawyer, June 22, 1992: distinguished; capital actual-innocence gateway is not a universal limit on appellate correction.

#### Instructed alternates do not establish prejudice by presence alone

**Holding and operative rule:** For these alternates, instructed not to participate, mere presence during deliberations neither establishes prejudice nor warrants a presumption of prejudice under Rule 52(b). Without a specific showing of participation or a chilling effect, respondents have not shown an effect on substantial rights; the per se new-trial ruling must be reversed.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that deliberative privacy protects against improper influence, and the inquiry must attend to that purpose. Parker and Remmer examine intrusions for prejudice; Smith v. Phillips rejects a new trial merely because a juror encountered a potentially compromising situation. Those authorities do not make this Rule 24(c) violation harmless by definition, but they defeat treating every intrusion as automatically reversible. The alternates heard the same evidence, took the ordinary oath, and received an express direction not to participate. Richardson supports the ordinary assumption that jurors follow instructions. Respondents showed neither actual participation, including nonverbal influence, nor restraint of the regular jurors’ discussion, and the circumstances do not justify presuming either. The Court does not decide the Government’s burden had an objection been preserved. It also reserves what Rule 606(b) would permit at a hearing and whether an appellate court may order such a hearing on plain-error review; none was requested here.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Parker v. Gladden, 385 U.S. 363, and Remmer v. United States, 347 U.S. 227: apply the focus on prejudicial influence, preserving appropriate presumptions in other circumstances.
- Smith v. Phillips, 455 U.S. 209: applies rejection of automatic retrial for every compromising contact.
- Richardson v. Marsh, 481 U.S. 200: applies the ordinary instruction-following presumption, not an irrebuttable cure.
- Simulated Zafiro, January 25, 1993: consistent record-specific instruction analysis; no blanket rule that instructions cure intrusions.

**Before and after / present force:** Before: Rule52(b) and Atkinson/Young supplied discretionary correction without this complete articulation. After: four separate requirements govern forfeited-error review; ordinary defendant prejudice burden and the no-presumed-prejudice result for instructed alternates apply. Valid waiver, new clarity on appeal, structural categories and Rule606(b)/hearing authority remain reserved. Brecht’s Chapman rule for preserved Doyle habeas error and Zafiro’s record-specific instruction analysis remain unchanged.

#### United States v. California — 1993-04-26

#### Reimbursement does not create the asserted federal refund right

**Holding and operative rule:** Federal reimbursement or advance funding of an independent contractor’s state taxes does not alone create a federal common-law action for money had and received to recover alleged state-law assessment errors. The contractor’s legal tax liability does not become a direct tax on the United States merely because the Treasury bears its economic cost.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the source of the money does not establish the source of the asserted right. New Mexico separates a tax on the Government itself from a tax on an independent contractor whose expenses the Government pays. The same distinction applies to the attempt to create a refund obligation here. Brady likewise rejects using a private indemnity arrangement to change a third party’s legal rights. California demanded a debt from WBEC; WBEC neither stole the federal money nor stood in a relationship making California responsible for a wrongful diversion. Bayne and Gaines therefore do not supply an implied obligation to the United States: those cases involved unlawful taking and legally responsible recipients. The Government’s financial interest is substantial, but it cannot alone transform a state-law tax dispute into this federal restitution action. The Court leaves direct federal taxation and any remedy Congress creates outside the holding and does not decide whether California correctly classified the purchases.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. New Mexico, 455 U.S. 720: applies legal incidence/economic burden distinction.
- Brady v. Roosevelt S.S. Co., 317 U.S. 575: applies limits on altering third-party rights by indemnity.
- Bayne v. United States, 93 U.S. 642, and Gaines v. Miller, 111 U.S. 395: distinguished; unlawful diversion and recipient responsibility are absent.

#### Later subrogation does not revive the expired claim

**Holding and operative rule:** Although indemnification gave the Government a right to seek subrogation, applicable California law required active assertion before substitution occurred; by the May 1988 federal suit, WBEC’s remaining refund claim had lapsed. The United States takes that derivative claim subject to its pre-existing infirmity; the Court does not decide generally whether state or federal limitations govern state-law claims brought by the United States.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that acquiring the opportunity to seek substitution is not the same as acquiring an enforceable claim free of existing defenses. Munsey Trust states the ordinary rule that a subrogee stands in the place of the party whose obligation it paid. Under Offer, payment furnished a right to seek subrogation, but active assertion was required to perfect that substitution. WBEC’s timely actions ended when it dismissed them without prejudice, and it could not commence a new refund action by the time the United States invoked subrogation. Guaranty Trust resolves the resulting problem: federal protection from state limitation periods does not revive a right already impaired when acquired. Summerlin protects a live federal claim from subsequent state restrictions and remains intact. The Government’s argument therefore fails on the condition of this derivative claim, not on a general rule subjecting sovereign claims to state deadlines. The completed partial refund remains untouched.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Munsey Trust Co., 332 U.S. 234: applies derivative nature of subrogation.
- Offer v. Superior Court, 194 Cal.114: applies the state-law active-assertion requirement.
- Guaranty Trust Co. v. United States, 304 U.S. 126: applies pre-acquisition infirmity rule.
- United States v. Summerlin, 310 U.S. 414: distinguished and preserved for live federal claims against subsequent state limits.

**Before and after / present force:** Before and after: legal incidence is distinct from Treasury reimbursement; subrogation transfers only a live derivative right. These principles defeat the two asserted recovery theories. No new general limitations rule, federal refund cause, tax-classification ruling or treatment of direct federal taxation is created.

#### Edenfield v. Fane — 1993-04-26

#### Real advancement must be established in the relevant professional setting

**Holding and operative rule:** When truthful, nonmisleading solicitation concerns lawful CPA services, the State must demonstrate that the harms supporting its restriction are real and that the restriction alleviates them to a material degree. Florida’s unsupported predictions about direct solicitation of business clients do not satisfy that burden; the ruling neither requires empirical studies in every case nor immunizes deceptive or coercive solicitation.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the State must connect its legitimate concerns to the speech it suppresses. Central Hudson requires more than a substantial interest stated in the abstract; Fox preserves a reasonable fit rather than demanding the least restrictive imaginable rule. Florida’s interests in preventing fraud, protecting privacy and maintaining independence are substantial, but the record does not establish the asserted threat from CPA business solicitation. The Board’s generalized affidavit does not show that such contacts undermine professional judgment, and the professional evidence points the other way. Ohralik permitted prophylaxis against personal solicitation by lawyers in circumstances involving vulnerable clients and pressure for immediate commitment. Business executives considering accounting services can control the time and setting and need not decide immediately. That difference defeats the proposed analogy, not every professional prophylactic rule. Nor can labeling the prohibition a time, place and manner restriction cure its failure materially to advance the asserted interests, even assuming that alternative framework applies. Fraud, coercion and materially different settings remain open.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corp. v. Public Service Commission, 447 U.S.557: applies the real-advancement burden.
- Board of Trustees v. Fox, 492 U.S.469: retains reasonable fit without least restrictive means.
- Ohralik v. Ohio State Bar Assn., 436 U.S.447: distinguished by professional setting, vulnerability and pressure.
- Ward v. Rock Against Racism, 491 U.S.781: alternative time/place/manner characterization cannot cure deficient advancement.
- Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, March 24, 1993, simulated: consistent application; no expansion to strict scrutiny.

**Before and after / present force:** Before: Discovery Network, Central Hudson and Fox require real advancement and reasonable fit. After: those requirements invalidate this unsupported CPA business-solicitation prohibition; no strict scrutiny, universal studies requirement or least-restrictive-means rule is adopted. Fraud, coercion and materially different professional settings remain regulable.

#### United States v. Idaho ex rel. Director, Idaho Department of Water Resources — 1993-05-03

#### Adjudicatory consent does not authorize these monetary assessments

**Holding and operative rule:** The McCarran Amendment subjects the United States to the state procedures governing the water adjudication, but does not specifically waive immunity from Idaho’s challenged filing assessments. Its express exclusion of judgments for costs and the blurred line between these assessments and former costs reinforce that conclusion; participation in the adjudication does not itself create liability for the charges.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that consent to adjudicate water rights does not itself consent to this monetary exaction. Eagle County gives the McCarran Amendment its intended comprehensive reach, so the Government cannot escape ordinary state procedural requirements merely by calling them nonsubstantive. That conclusion does not answer the distinct question of payment. Shaw and the monetary-consent principle applied in Nordic Village require attention to the obligation Congress actually authorized. Section666 contains no specific authorization for these charges and expressly prohibits judgments for costs. Idaho’s system collects in advance expenses formerly taxed at the end of adjudication, blurring the ordinary distinction between fees and costs. That history reinforces the absence of sufficiently specific monetary consent; the Court need not classify every fee as a cost. The State’s practical financing interest cannot enlarge the waiver. The United States remains a party whose water rights may be determined under the applicable law. The Court does not announce immunity from every procedural requirement or decide charges imposed under a different express waiver.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. District Court in and for Eagle County, 401 U.S.520: comprehensive adjudicatory waiver preserved, not converted to universal payment consent.
- Library of Congress v. Shaw, 478 U.S.310: applies specificity of monetary consent.
- United States v. Nordic Village, February25 1992, simulated: particular monetary-waiver principle applied by analogy; bankruptcy holding unchanged.

**Before and after / present force:** Before: adjudicatory waiver and specific monetary consent were distinct. After: §666 reaches ordinary state adjudicatory procedure but not these filing assessments under its nonspecific monetary language and express costs protection. The seven-Justice rationale controls; Stone and Stevens’ definitive costs characterization is a separate, noncontrolling ground. Federal water-rights participation continues.

#### Cisneros v. Alpine Ridge Group — 1993-05-03

#### The comparability limit overrides an unconditional factor entitlement

**Holding and operative rule:** The contracts’ automatic annual adjustment mechanism is subject to §1.9(d)’s overriding prohibition on material differences from comparable unassisted rents, while preserving differences existing at the initial contract date and the protected initial contract rent floor. HUD may use independent comparability studies to enforce that limit; the Court does not determine whether any particular study accurately and lawfully does so.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an adjustment formula cannot be read apart from the contract’s express limitation on its results. Section1.9(b) establishes the annual-factor mechanism, but section1.9(d) applies notwithstanding other provisions and therefore limits the entitlement that mechanism produces. Shomberg supplies the ordinary effect of that overriding language. The owners’ proposed exclusive-formula reading would deprive the comparability provision of practical force whenever the formula itself created a material difference. Neither the contract nor the statutory structure requires that result. An independent study can identify the disparity the limitation addresses; HUD need not rely solely on revising a general factor. This authority does not erase the proviso preserving differences existing when the contract began or permit a reduction below the protected initial rent. Accuracy, comparability and faithful implementation remain contestable. Because the owners possess no unconditional contractual right to the disputed factor increases, the constitutional impairment ruling lacks its asserted predicate. The Court does not otherwise decide the validity of every application of the 1989 legislation.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Shomberg v. United States, 348 U.S.540: applies the overriding effect of notwithstanding language.
- Rainier View Associates v. United States, 848 F.2d988: rejects the lower-court exclusive-factor construction; no Supreme Court precedent overruled.

**Before and after / present force:** Before: the whole HAP contract governed; an unconditional factor entitlement was asserted below. After: independent comparability studies are permitted within the overriding material-difference limit, initial-difference proviso and initial-rent floor. Study accuracy remains open; the constitutional impairment ruling cannot rest on the rejected unconditional entitlement.

#### Stinson v. United States — 1993-05-03

#### Consistent interpretive commentary is authoritative

**Holding and operative rule:** Guidelines commentary interpreting or explaining a guideline is authoritative unless it violates the Constitution or a federal statute, or is inconsistent with, or a plainly erroneous reading of, that guideline. Commentary cannot amend contrary guideline text; a prior judicial interpretation does not alone defeat a later, permissible Commission interpretation.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court; White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Commission’s permissible explanation of its guideline carries more than advisory force. Mistretta establishes the Commission’s delegated role in the sentencing system. Section3553(b) expressly gives commentary a role in identifying matters the Commission considered, and section1B1.7 explains its interpretive function. Williams confirms the authority of a prohibitory departure policy within that structure; the present decision addresses interpretive commentary more broadly. Seminole Rock provides the appropriate analogy because the Commission explains a rule it promulgated. The absence of congressional review for commentary amendments does not make every explanation optional, but it does preserve the boundary between interpretation and amendment. A contrary statute, constitutional command or guideline controls, and a plainly erroneous reading receives no authority. The Eleventh Circuit’s earlier construction is therefore not a sufficient reason to disregard a later consistent explanation. This holding concerns interpretive and explanatory commentary; it does not allow the Commission to change binding text through an inconsistent note or decide whether every later explanation applies to every earlier sentence.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Mistretta v. United States, 488 U.S.361: applies the delegated institutional framework.
- Bowles v. Seminole Rock & Sand Co., 325 U.S.410: extends consistency-limited interpretive authority to Guidelines commentary.
- Williams v. United States, March9 1992, simulated: preserves prohibitory-policy authority and extends analysis to interpretive commentary without altering §3742(f) review.

#### The possession exclusion is a permissible interpretation, with temporal application reserved

**Holding and operative rule:** Amendment433’s exclusion of unlawful firearm possession by a felon is a permissible interpretation of the crime-of-violence guideline and controls defendants to whom it applies. The appellate judgment sustaining a categorical possession rule is vacated; the governing-version and temporal objection remains for remand, together with properly presented independent sentencing grounds and the consequences of any guideline error.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court; White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Commission’s possession exclusion fits within the guideline it explains. The crime-of-violence definition includes a force clause and a serious-potential-risk clause, with listed offenses informing the latter. Possession by a prohibited person does not necessarily involve force, and treating possession alone as outside the definition is not an inconsistent or plainly erroneous construction of that risk language. Under the interpretive-authority rule adopted today, the appellate court could not preserve its categorical contrary construction merely because it preceded the commentary. That error requires vacating its judgment. It does not establish that every defendant sentenced earlier receives the benefit of every later amendment. The Government’s distinct temporal argument was not resolved below and lies beyond the authority question reviewed, so the lower court must address it in identifying the applicable rule. Williams continues to govern the remedial consequences of an incorrect guideline application. The Court orders neither a specified replacement sentence nor reopening of final cases, and does not treat the other counts as automatically sufficient alternative predicates.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Seminole Rock, 325 U.S.410: applies the consistency test to Amendment433.
- Williams, March9 1992, simulated: preserves statutory error and remand analysis; no automatic replacement sentence.

**Before and after / present force:** Before: Williams established authority for express prohibitory departure policy and statutory error review, not all interpretive commentary. After: eight Justices adopt authoritative, consistency-limited interpretive commentary and uphold Amendment433 as a permissible interpretation. Temporal applicability and lawful sentencing alternatives remain open. Stone’s persuasive-only approach is noncontrolling; Williams error consequences remain.

#### Professional Real Estate Investors, Inc. v. Columbia Pictures Industries, Inc. — 1993-05-03

#### A single suit must be objectively baseless before motive defeats petitioning protection

**Holding and operative rule:** Under the sham theory presented, a lawsuit loses Noerr protection only if it is objectively baseless, so that no reasonable litigant could realistically expect success on the merits, and then is shown to use the governmental process itself, rather than its outcome, as an anticompetitive weapon. The copyright suit had an objectively reasonable basis, ending this inquiry without motive discovery; even a sham finding would leave the ordinary antitrust elements to be proved.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an unsuccessful suit is not a sham merely because its filer sought a competitive advantage. Noerr and Pennington protect efforts to obtain government action, and Omni distinguishes the requested result from abuse of the process itself. California Motor Transport preserves that latter inquiry; it does not permit an adverse motive alone to strip a reasonably grounded suit of protection. Bill Johnson’s likewise separates baseless litigation from reasonably founded claims. The studios’ copyright theory addressed an unsettled public-performance question and presented a realistic prospect of success, even though it ultimately failed. Objective probable cause therefore defeats the sham theory here without discovery into motive or an inquiry into whether an expected victory would be economically profitable. Only after objective baselessness is established does the process-versus-outcome inquiry arise. The Court does not resolve alleged fraud in obtaining government action, a pattern of serial proceedings or independently unlawful conduct outside this theory. Nor does loss of immunity establish antitrust liability without its substantive elements.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Eastern Railroad Presidents Conference v. Noerr Motor Freight, Inc., 365 U.S.127, and United Mine Workers v. Pennington, 381 U.S.657: applies protection for government-directed competitive advocacy.
- City of Columbia v. Omni Outdoor Advertising, Inc., 499 U.S.365: applies process/outcome distinction.
- California Motor Transport Co. v. Trucking Unlimited, 404 U.S.508: clarifies sham litigation without deciding every pattern or fraud case.
- Bill Johnson’s Restaurants, Inc. v. NLRB, 461 U.S.731: applies distinction between baseless and reasonably grounded suits.
- Spectrum Sports, January25 1993, simulated: ordinary antitrust elements remain necessary; no new exemption from their proof.

**Before and after / present force:** Before: Noerr protected governmental petitioning with a sham exception. After: the reviewed single-suit theory requires objective baselessness before subjective process abuse; the objectively reasonable copyright suit fails that gate. Fraud, serial proceedings and independent conduct are not resolved, and ordinary antitrust elements remain necessary.

#### United States v. Padilla — 1993-05-03

#### Suppression depends on the defendant’s own protected interest

**Holding and operative rule:** A defendant’s participation in a conspiracy neither creates nor defeats a personal Fourth Amendment claim: the defendant must show interference with his or her own protected property interest in a seizure or reasonable expectation of privacy in a search. The conspiracy exception is rejected, and the lower court must evaluate each reviewed defendant’s actual interests without reopening the unchallenged illegality of the stop.

**Authority:** Per curiam Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that association in criminal activity cannot substitute for a personal constitutional injury. Alderman expressly rejects extending suppression rights merely because defendants are coconspirators. Rakas, Salvucci and Rawlings likewise require a defendant to identify the protected interest that the challenged official conduct invaded. The Ninth Circuit’s exception departed from that rule by treating a conspiratorial relationship as an independent source of standing. Actual ownership, control or a privacy interest may matter, but the conspiracy label itself does not. Jacobsen distinguishes interference with possession from invasion of privacy, and Soldal preserves that distinction in the home-seizure setting; an individualized inquiry must therefore consider the type of official conduct at issue. The Court does not decide that every respondent lacks such an interest, or that the evidence must be admitted against all of them. The Government has not challenged the unlawful-stop determination. Strubbe’s unpetitioned loss also remains untouched. Remand is required to assess the remaining defendants under the ordinary personal-rights rule.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Alderman v. United States, 394 U.S.165: applies express rejection of vicarious coconspirator rights.
- Rakas v. Illinois, 439 U.S.128; United States v. Salvucci, 448 U.S.83; Rawlings v. Kentucky, 448 U.S.98: applies individualized protected-interest inquiry.
- United States v. Jacobsen, 466 U.S.109: preserves distinct search and seizure interests.
- Soldal v. Cook County, December8 1992, simulated: consistent possessory/privacy distinction; home holding not enlarged into categorical vehicle standing.

**Before and after / present force:** Before and after: personal Fourth Amendment interests, with search and seizure distinguished, control suppression. Conspiracy creates no exception and destroys no actual right; individual evaluation remains on remand. Soldal’s home-specific holding is preserved.

#### Cardinal Chemical Co. v. Morton International, Inc. — 1993-05-17

#### An intermediate noninfringement ruling does not terminate counterclaim jurisdiction

**Holding and operative rule:** The Federal Circuit’s affirmance of noninfringement does not deprive it of jurisdiction over a live, independent invalidity counterclaim merely because its own ruling would resolve the infringement claim. That intermediate ruling remains subject to this Court’s review and does not justify treating validity review as automatically advisory.

**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an intermediate appellate court cannot treat its own noninfringement ruling as the final, unreviewable end of the litigation. Its judgment remains subject to this Court’s review. A reversal of noninfringement could restore the immediate importance of the independently adjudicated validity issue, so the appellate court’s own ruling cannot by itself remove authority to review that issue. Altvater confirms that a counterclaim seeking a declaration of invalidity presents more than a defense to the particular infringement claim. Electrical Fittings does not support the automatic rule: that case involved an unnecessary validity adjudication in rejecting infringement, not an independent counterclaim judgment requiring appellate treatment. Judicial caution about unnecessary questions cannot convert the Federal Circuit’s merits decision into a jurisdictional event beyond further review. This is an independently sufficient reason to reject the automatic mootness premise. The Court does not disturb the noninfringement affirmance here, decide technical validity or foreclose actual mootness arising from a different event.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Altvater v. Freeman, 319 U.S.359: applies independent-counterclaim distinction.
- Electrical Fittings Corp. v. Thomas & Betts Co., 307 U.S.241: distinguished; unnecessary affirmative-defense adjudication is different.

#### An established invalidity controversy does not disappear without a change

**Holding and operative rule:** Once an independent invalidity counterclaim presents an actual controversy, the party asserting later mootness must demonstrate a change that eliminates it. Affirmance of noninfringement alone does not establish that change or authorize automatic vacatur of the invalidity judgment.

**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the distinct validity controversy continues unless something actually ends it. Aetna places the initial burden of establishing a real controversy on the party seeking declaratory relief. That threshold was met here. W.T.Grant assigns the burden differently when a party contends that an established controversy has later become moot. The automatic practice identifies no new fact ending the dispute about the patents’ validity. Altvater explains why an invalidity counterclaim remains distinct from a defense to infringement: success on the particular infringement allegation need not resolve the parties’ continuing patent controversy. Munsingwear addresses vacatur after genuine intervening mootness; it does not supply the missing intervening event here. Both parties ask for validity review but disagree about the result, so their agreement on the need for review does not eliminate adversity, as Chadha’s treatment of agreement on a legal issue confirms. The Court leaves genuine later mootness available and does not decide validity itself.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Altvater v. Freeman, 319 U.S.359: applies independent-counterclaim distinction.
- Aetna Life Insurance Co. v. Haworth, 300 U.S.227, and United States v. W.T.Grant Co., 345 U.S.629: distinguishes initial controversy burden from later mootness burden.
- United States v. Munsingwear, Inc., 340 U.S.36: actual intervening mootness rule preserved; automatic application rejected.
- INS v. Chadha, 462 U.S.919: agreement on one legal issue does not eliminate underlying adversity.

#### The automatic practice cannot be saved as discretion on this record

**Holding and operative rule:** The Federal Circuit may not routinely vacate a live invalidity judgment merely because it affirms noninfringement, nor save that same automatic practice by calling it discretionary. On this record the validity appeal should be considered; unusual case-specific grounds for declining review or actual mootness remain available, but no universal duty to decide validity in every patent appeal is imposed.

**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that jurisdiction cannot be acknowledged while the same automatic refusal is preserved under another name. The validity counterclaim has consequences beyond whether the particular accused products infringe. Blonder-Tongue explains why an adjudication of invalidity can protect others from repeated enforcement of a patent that should not restrict the public. Routine vacatur also prevents review of the district court’s work and can force the prevailing accused infringer to confront the same validity controversy again. The patent holder has sought review as well, so this record presents no reason to preserve a categorical refusal. Judicial restraint is important, but it does not justify a standing practice that disregards a live independent judgment whenever a separate ground resolves infringement. The Federal Circuit should consider the validity appeal on remand. The Court does not eliminate lawful case-specific discretion, dictate the technical validity result or require unnecessary constitutional adjudication. An unusual case presenting a genuine reason to withhold review must be assessed on its own circumstances.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Blonder-Tongue Laboratories, Inc. v. University of Illinois Foundation, 402 U.S.313: applies distinct public and preclusion significance of invalidity.
- Altvater, 319 U.S.359: independent judgment cannot be discarded solely because infringement fails.

**Before and after / present force:** Before: independent invalidity counterclaims had distinct legal consequences. After: the automatic mootness-and-vacatur practice is rejected on two nine-Justice jurisdictional grounds; seven Justices also require consideration on this record rather than automatic avoidance relabeled discretion. Genuine later mootness and lawful unusual-case discretion remain. No patent validity is decided.

#### McNeil v. United States — 1993-05-17

[Canonical Decision Record](records/mcneil_merits_1993-05-17.md). Affirmed, 9–0.

#### Administrative exhaustion must precede the original FTCA action

**Holding and operative rule:** An FTCA claimant must present the claim to the appropriate federal agency and receive its final written denial, sent by certified or registered mail, before instituting the action; after six months without final disposition, the claimant may instead elect to treat the inaction as final denial. Exhaustion achieved after filing does not cure the original premature action, even when little litigation has occurred; §2675(a) expressly excepts claims asserted under the Federal Rules by third-party complaint, crossclaim or counterclaim.

**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statute fixes the order in which the claimant and the courts must proceed. Presenting a claim and awaiting the agency’s disposition are steps preceding institution of the action, not conditions that may be completed while it remains pending. Hallstrom enforces a comparable statutory sequence and rejects an invitation to replace it with an assessment of practical progress in the litigation. McCarthy preserves the primacy of an express congressional command, while excusing a different, judicially imposed exhaustion requirement where Congress supplied none. Administrative settlement and orderly processing also require a rule that does not turn on how quickly the parties litigate after an unlawful filing. McNeil’s lack of counsel does not authorize a different sequence: Haines concerns reading pleadings fairly, not dispensing with statutory procedure. This conclusion addresses the original action alone. The Court does not decide whether later papers commenced another action or how any limitations defense to that action should be resolved.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Hallstrom v. Tillamook County, 493 U.S.20: applied to enforce a congressionally fixed prefiling sequence.
- McCarthy v. Madigan, March 4, 1992: statutory primacy applied; its damages-only BOP exception is distinguished.
- Haines v. Kerner, 404 U.S.519: liberal pleading construction preserved; it does not excuse the prefiling requirement.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: §2675(a) required prior administrative presentation and exhaustion. After: the Court rejects a no-substantial-progress cure of the original premature action. The six-month election and third-party-complaint, crossclaim and counterclaim exceptions remain; new-action and limitations questions remain open.

#### Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Sac and Fox Nation — 1993-05-17

[Canonical Decision Record](records/sac_fox_merits_1993-05-17.md). Vacated and remanded, 9–0, on the reviewed member income and vehicle-tax components.

#### Member income protection depends on Indian-country residence and source

**Holding and operative rule:** Absent express congressional authorization, a State may not tax a tribal member’s income earned in Indian country when the member resides there; tribal employment alone does not establish that protection. The residence inquiry includes §1151 reservations, dependent Indian communities and allotments whose Indian titles have not been extinguished, including rights-of-way, rather than only an undiminished formal reservation.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the place where a tribal member lives is an indispensable part of the inquiry. McClanahan protected a member who both lived and earned the relevant income on the reservation. The lower court therefore could not extend that result to every member employee merely because the employer was the Tribe. But Oklahoma’s competing reservation-only rule is also mistaken. Congress’s Indian-country definition includes dependent communities and qualifying allotments, and Potawatomi confirms the significance of federally protected tribal land beyond a formal reservation label. The residence inquiry can consequently be resolved on an applicable allotment without deciding every contested reservation boundary. Oklahoma identifies no express congressional permission for these taxes; its failure to assume jurisdiction under the applicable Public Law 280 procedures supplies none. Yakima concerns a different, expressly authorized fee-land levy. The Court remands for the missing residence predicates and leaves open any independently supported protection of tribal self-government for members living outside Indian country.

**Precedent treatment:**

- McClanahan v. Arizona State Tax Commission, 411 U.S.164: applied with both residence and income-source predicates.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe, 498 U.S.505: applied against a reservation-label-only geography.
- County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes, January 14, 1992: distinguished; limited land-tax authorization supplies no income-tax authority.

#### The challenged vehicle levies cannot be sustained merely as off-country use charges

**Holding and operative rule:** For tribal members residing in Indian country, the challenged vehicle excise and registration levies fall within the prohibition on state property taxation absent congressional authorization; collecting the same forbidden charges from later purchasers does not save them. Their application to the established residents must be reconsidered on remand; the Court does not decide a genuinely apportioned off-country road-use charge or every tax connected to a vehicle sale.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the substance of these levies, not their labels, controls. Moe and Colville prohibit the state vehicle property taxes imposed on protected resident tribal members. Oklahoma’s excise is distinct from its sales tax, and the registration levy is imposed in place of personal property taxation. The State has not shown that either assessment measures the amount of travel outside Indian country. Exempting nonresident vehicles further weakens the claim that these are simply charges for using the roads. Requiring a later purchaser to pay the accumulated amounts for the tribal-registration period burdens the same protected ownership and cannot accomplish indirectly what the State cannot do directly. The ruling nevertheless requires the resident-member predicate absent from the lower court’s analysis. It does not transform tribal registration into immunity for every owner, invalidate a demonstrated and properly tailored road-use charge, or decide the different position of nonmembers.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Moe v. Confederated Salish and Kootenai Tribes, 425 U.S.463: applied to member vehicle property taxation.
- Washington v. Confederated Tribes of the Colville Indian Reservation, 447 U.S.134: applied to the substance of the vehicle levies; genuinely tailored off-country use charges remain distinct.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before and after: congressional authorization, membership, residence and the particular tax remain separate inquiries. Indian country is not restricted to formal reservations. The reviewed judgment must be reconsidered under the actual residence predicates; the broader outside-residence self-government issue remains open. Yakima and Negonsott keep their distinct statutory scopes.

#### Deal v. United States — 1993-05-17

[Canonical Decision Record](records/deal_merits_1993-05-17.md). Affirmed, 5–4.

#### Additional convictions in the same prosecution trigger the enhanced tier

**Holding and operative rule:** Under the applicable §924(c)(1), a conviction means the finding of guilt; a second or subsequent conviction on a distinct firearm count may therefore occur in the same prosecution as the first. The statute does not require commission of the later offense after an earlier conviction, and the enhanced terms remain subject to its consecutive-service command.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court; White, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statute counts convictions, not prosecutions separated by an opportunity to reoffend. In this sentencing provision, conviction denotes the finding of guilt rather than a final judgment already including sentence. Each distinct finding after the first is an additional conviction even when the findings arise in the same proceeding; making the sentence part of the predicate would confuse what triggers punishment with the punishment being imposed. The dissent’s recidivist construction supplies an intervening-offense condition that Congress did not write. Bell and the Court’s earlier firearm cases require attention to ambiguity in the enacted scheme, but they do not direct courts to disregard a meaning settled by this context. The seriousness of the resulting sentence does not establish textual uncertainty or authorize a different statutory sequence. Deal’s six findings thus support one base term and five enhanced terms. The Court does not decide a constitutional proportionality claim or the operation of later legislation.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Bell v. United States, 349 U.S.81: residual lenity preserved; no unresolved ambiguity found in this sentencing sequence.
- Simpson v. United States, 435 U.S.6: distinguished; cumulative statutory punishment question does not settle the present conviction sequence.
- Busic v. United States, 446 U.S.398: distinguished on the same limited basis; its earlier statutory problem is not reopened.
- United States v. Thompson/Center Arms Co., June 8, 1992: no general new lenity method displaced; its different statutory ambiguity does not control this phrase.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: the same-prosecution reach of the enhanced tier was open in this Court. After: five Justices authorize it for distinct additional convictions under the applicable statute. Neither dissent is current law. Convictions, consecutive service and the unreviewed sentence remain distinct; no appellate-finality or proportionality rule is announced.

#### El Vocero de Puerto Rico v. Puerto Rico — 1993-05-17

[Canonical Decision Record](records/el_vocero_summary_merits_1993-05-17.md). Certiorari granted; judgment reversed and case remanded by reasoned per curiam opinion, 9–0.

#### Trial-like preliminary hearings require qualified public access

**Holding and operative rule:** The First Amendment’s qualified right of access applies to these adversarial preliminary hearings; the history inquiry concerns this type of proceeding, not only Puerto Rico’s own practice. Closure requires specific findings supporting an overriding interest and a narrowly tailored order after reasonable alternatives are considered; where fair-trial prejudice is invoked, there must be a substantial probability of prejudice that closure would prevent and no adequate reasonable alternative.

**Authority:** Per curiam Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a defendant’s preference cannot replace the constitutional justification for closing a judicial proceeding. Press-Enterprise II applies experience and function to preliminary hearings, and these hearings contain the adversarial safeguards that make public observation useful: a neutral decisionmaker, counsel, cross-examination and an opportunity to present a defense. Globe Newspaper rejects a categorical substitute for the particularized inquiry. A jurisdiction cannot defeat that inquiry merely by pointing to its own tradition of closure, for the relevant experience concerns the kind of hearing across the country. Fair-trial concerns remain substantial, but their existence must be demonstrated with the required probability of prejudice and inadequacy of reasonable alternatives. Witness safety can likewise support a properly justified and limited protective order. Rule 23(c)’s default therefore cannot stand. The Court does not establish access to every pretrial activity, open grand-jury proceedings, or order disclosure of material covered by a valid narrow protection.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Richmond Newspapers, Inc. v. Virginia, 448 U.S.555: access’s public oversight function applied.
- Globe Newspaper Co. v. Superior Court, 457 U.S.596: categorical closure rejected in favor of a particularized justification.
- Press-Enterprise Co. v. Superior Court (II), 478 U.S.1: trial-like preliminary-hearing access and the fair-trial closure requirements applied.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before and after: qualified access, not absolute access, governs. The framework is applied to Puerto Rico’s trial-like preliminary hearings; local closure tradition and an accused’s election cannot replace specific justification. The express remand supplies implementation without deciding a particular protective order.

#### Commissioner v. Keystone Consolidated Industries, Inc. — 1993-05-24

[Canonical Decision Record](records/keystone_merits_1993-05-24.md). Reversed and remanded, 8–1; Stevens dissents.

#### The established tax meaning includes property exchanged for release of a funding duty

**Holding and operative rule:** In §4975(c)(1)(A), the established tax meaning of sale or exchange includes an employer’s transfer of property in satisfaction of an enforceable pension-funding obligation. An unencumbered transfer meeting that condition is not excluded merely because it is called a contribution.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Part III-A; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress used a familiar tax expression against an established legal background. Hammel recognizes that sale or exchange can encompass a disposition discharging an obligation, even when the transaction does not resemble a voluntary cash sale. Sorenson supports reading repeated tax language consistently where the statutory setting warrants it. Keystone receives release from its funding obligation in exchange for the property transferred, and indeed treated the disposition as a sale or exchange in reporting its own income-tax consequences. Atlantic Cleaners warns against mechanically transferring a meaning between different settings, but the prohibited-transaction provision’s breadth supplies no reason to exclude this exchange. The dissent’s concern about voluntary contributions does not alter the consideration furnished when an existing obligation is discharged. This rationale independently supports classification of the reviewed transfers. It does not decide every gratuitous contribution, settle an unpresented exemption, or calculate the remaining excise-tax consequences.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Helvering v. Hammel, 311 U.S.504: settled tax treatment of obligation-discharging transfers applied.
- Sorenson v. Secretary of Treasury, 475 U.S.851: consistent tax-language construction applied.
- Atlantic Cleaners & Dyers, Inc. v. United States, 286 U.S.427: contextual qualification preserved; it does not justify a different meaning here.
- Commissioner v. Lester, 366 U.S.299: internal consistency of tax language supports the established-meaning rationale.
- Albernaz v. United States, 450 U.S.333: prior settled construction informs the meaning Congress selected.

#### Discharge supplies consideration even without importing a technical tax meaning

**Holding and operative rule:** Independently of a settled technical tax meaning, transferring property to discharge an employer’s pension-funding duty is an exchange under §4975(c)(1)(A): the plan receives property and the employer receives release from its obligation. Section4975(f)(3)’s inclusion of property subject to a mortgage or similar lien assumed by the plan or placed by a disqualified person within ten years does not exclude other exchanges supported by consideration.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Parts III-C and III-D; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the actual legal effects of this transfer suffice even without relying on a specialized tax usage. The plan obtains property; the employer obtains satisfaction of a duty it otherwise must perform. Those reciprocal legal benefits constitute an exchange under the ordinary meaning of the enacted words. Keystone’s reading of the encumbrance provision mistakes an express inclusion for an exhaustive definition. Section4975(f)(3) ensures coverage when a plan assumes a mortgage or similar lien or the disqualified person placed the lien within the stated ten-year period; it does not say that a transfer discharging a separate funding obligation is excluded. The statutory text thus answers the strongest structural objection without relying on policy hostility toward property funding. The Court classifies the transfers presented and leaves the application of any properly available exemption, correction provision and remaining tax requirement to its own conditions. It makes no new finding about the property’s value.

**Precedent treatment:**

- This independently sufficient ground rests directly on §4975(c)(1)(A) and (f)(3); it requires no additional treatment of an earlier decision.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: the specific transfer classification was unresolved in this Court. After: seven Justices adopt the tax-context ground; eight adopt the independently sufficient ordinary-exchange ground and reject an exclusive encumbrance definition. The six-Justice policy discussion supports but does not enlarge those rules. No deference holding is necessary; the Court reserves agency-deference questions. Exemption, correction and tax-computation conditions remain distinct.

#### United States Department of Justice v. Landano — 1993-05-24

[Canonical Decision Record](records/landano_merits_1993-05-24.md). Vacated and remanded, 9–0.

#### Confidentiality requires an assurance or a supported inference, not an FBI label

**Holding and operative rule:** Under Exemption7(D), a source is confidential when the information was furnished under an express assurance or circumstances supporting a reasonable inference of confidentiality; an assurance of absolute secrecy is unnecessary. The Government bears the burden and cannot presume every FBI criminal-investigation source confidential, but may rely on a supported, rebuttable inference tied to particular circumstances or a properly defined category, including the crime and the source’s relationship to it.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.
